I think… I think it’s both in a way, but the part that scares me most is the not being able to stop. That’s what brings me here, really. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I’m planning it, no. I don’t sit down in the morning and think, “At three o’clock I’m going to eat this or that.” It doesn’t feel that organized, if that makes sense.

I do think about food in a regular way, you know, like what I need from the store or what I might make, because I’ve always cooked, and cooking has been part of my life for so long. But I don’t know that I spend a lot of time planning when I’ll eat. It’s more that food is just… around in my mind because it’s familiar. I notice it more when I’m alone, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’m making plans for it ahead of time.

It’s like there’s this little argument going on inside me, and the sensible part of me is losing. So yes, I may be upset or lonely, but in the moment it’s that loss of control that really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about food or planning when you will eat?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m planning it, no. I don’t sit down in the morning and think, “At three o’clock I’m going to eat this or that.” It doesn’t feel that organized, if that makes sense.\n\nI do think about food in a regular way, you know, like what I need from the store or what I might make, because I’ve always cooked, and cooking has been part of my life for so long. But I don’t know that I spend a lot of time planning when I’ll eat. It’s more that food is just… around in my mind because it’s familiar. I notice it more when I’m alone, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’m making plans for it ahead of time.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not really sure, Doctor. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very often—honestly, I tend to avoid it because it makes me nervous and then I start thinking about it too much. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I usually feel guilty every time, no. I mean, if it’s just a normal meal, like I make myself some toast or have supper, I don’t sit there afterward thinking I’ve done something terrible. At least not most of the time.

Sometimes I might feel a little bothered if I think I ate more than I meant to, even if it wasn’t one of those times where I truly couldn’t stop. But guilt… I’m not sure I’d call it that every time. It’s more like second-guessing myself, maybe. I’ll think, “Did I really need that?” or “Why did I have another serving?” But then other times I just eat and go on with the rest of the evening.

So I guess the answer is, not usually, not after ordinary eating. It’s more confusing than that.

I’m not sure I’d say that happens often, no. I mean, I suppose everybody criticizes themselves a little bit, don’t they? Especially when you get older and you look back on things, you can think, “Well, I could’ve done that better,” or “Maybe I should’ve handled that differently.” I do that sometimes.

But feeling like I’m not good enough in general… I don’t know. That’s a hard one to answer. I’m not someone who goes around thinking highly of myself, but I also don’t think I spend all day tearing myself down about everything. If I forget something or don’t get something done the way I wanted, I might get annoyed with myself, but it usually passes.

So I guess I’d say not especially, or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big pattern outside of the eating stuff. It’s more just ordinary second-guessing, I think.

I feel ashamed… and sad, too. It’s not only that I ate too much, it’s what it means to me, I suppose. Some of those foods used to feel like family, like caring for people, bringing a dish to church or making something the way my mother did. And now, when it happens like that, it feels like I’ve turned it into something secret and ugly, something I’m hiding from people at church.\n\nI’ll sit there afterward and think, “What is wrong with me, Maria?” And then I feel embarrassed, like if anyone knew, they’d look at me differently. It makes me feel very low.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating too much because you can't control your eating, or is it more that you're eating to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it’s both in a way, but the part that scares me most is the not being able to stop. That’s what brings me here, really. Once I start with those comfort foods, I can be standing right there and saying to myself, Maria, that is enough now. You don’t need any more. And I mean it when I say it. It’s not like I don’t know.\n\nBut then my hand goes back to the pot, or I take another bite, and another, and it feels like I can’t quite make myself stop even though I’m telling myself to. It’s like there’s this little argument going on inside me, and the sensible part of me is losing. So yes, I may be upset or lonely, but in the moment it’s that loss of control that really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about food or planning when you will eat?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m planning it, no. I don’t sit down in the morning and think, “At three o’clock I’m going to eat this or that.” It doesn’t feel that organized, if that makes sense.\n\nI do think about food in a regular way, you know, like what I need from the store or what I might make, because I’ve always cooked, and cooking has been part of my life for so long. But I don’t know that I spend a lot of time planning when I’ll eat. It’s more that food is just… around in my mind because it’s familiar. I notice it more when I’m alone, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’m making plans for it ahead of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, Doctor. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very often—honestly, I tend to avoid it because it makes me nervous and then I start thinking about it too much. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but I can’t say if that’s my weight changing or just, you know, how clothes fit depending on the day.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve noticed anything I’d call significant, but I wouldn’t want to say for certain without checking. I just haven’t been keeping track of it closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating, even when it's not one of those episodes where you feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I usually feel guilty every time, no. I mean, if it’s just a normal meal, like I make myself some toast or have supper, I don’t sit there afterward thinking I’ve done something terrible. At least not most of the time.\n\nSometimes I might feel a little bothered if I think I ate more than I meant to, even if it wasn’t one of those times where I truly couldn’t stop. But guilt… I’m not sure I’d call it that every time. It’s more like second-guessing myself, maybe. I’ll think, “Did I really need that?” or “Why did I have another serving?” But then other times I just eat and go on with the rest of the evening.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not usually, not after ordinary eating. It’s more confusing than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel like you're not good enough in areas of your life unrelated to eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often, no. I mean, I suppose everybody criticizes themselves a little bit, don’t they? Especially when you get older and you look back on things, you can think, “Well, I could’ve done that better,” or “Maybe I should’ve handled that differently.” I do that sometimes.\n\nBut feeling like I’m not good enough in general… I don’t know. That’s a hard one to answer. I’m not someone who goes around thinking highly of myself, but I also don’t think I spend all day tearing myself down about everything. If I forget something or don’t get something done the way I wanted, I might get annoyed with myself, but it usually passes.\n\nSo I guess I’d say not especially, or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big pattern outside of the eating stuff. It’s more just ordinary second-guessing, I think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not really sure I’d say I often worry about it. I mean, I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. At my age, your body isn’t the same as it used to be, and some days I’ll notice how something fits or I’ll catch sight of myself and think, “Well, I don’t like that very much.” But I don’t know that I sit around worrying about my shape all the time.

I do tend to avoid thinking too hard about my weight, honestly. Like I said, weighing myself makes me uneasy, so I don’t do it much. But that’s maybe more because I don’t want to get started down that road of thinking about numbers and whether it’s good or bad. It can make me uncomfortable.

So… I guess I’d put it more as I get bothered by it sometimes, or I’d rather not focus on it, but I don’t know if I’d call it a constant worry. It comes and goes.

I notice it more when I’m alone, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’m making plans for it ahead of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, Doctor. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very often—honestly, I tend to avoid it because it makes me nervous and then I start thinking about it too much. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but I can’t say if that’s my weight changing or just, you know, how clothes fit depending on the day.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve noticed anything I’d call significant, but I wouldn’t want to say for certain without checking. I just haven’t been keeping track of it closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating, even when it's not one of those episodes where you feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I usually feel guilty every time, no. I mean, if it’s just a normal meal, like I make myself some toast or have supper, I don’t sit there afterward thinking I’ve done something terrible. At least not most of the time.\n\nSometimes I might feel a little bothered if I think I ate more than I meant to, even if it wasn’t one of those times where I truly couldn’t stop. But guilt… I’m not sure I’d call it that every time. It’s more like second-guessing myself, maybe. I’ll think, “Did I really need that?” or “Why did I have another serving?” But then other times I just eat and go on with the rest of the evening.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not usually, not after ordinary eating. It’s more confusing than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel like you're not good enough in areas of your life unrelated to eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often, no. I mean, I suppose everybody criticizes themselves a little bit, don’t they? Especially when you get older and you look back on things, you can think, “Well, I could’ve done that better,” or “Maybe I should’ve handled that differently.” I do that sometimes.\n\nBut feeling like I’m not good enough in general… I don’t know. That’s a hard one to answer. I’m not someone who goes around thinking highly of myself, but I also don’t think I spend all day tearing myself down about everything. If I forget something or don’t get something done the way I wanted, I might get annoyed with myself, but it usually passes.\n\nSo I guess I’d say not especially, or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big pattern outside of the eating stuff. It’s more just ordinary second-guessing, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say I often worry about it. I mean, I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. At my age, your body isn’t the same as it used to be, and some days I’ll notice how something fits or I’ll catch sight of myself and think, “Well, I don’t like that very much.” But I don’t know that I sit around worrying about my shape all the time.\n\nI do tend to avoid thinking too hard about my weight, honestly. Like I said, weighing myself makes me uneasy, so I don’t do it much. But that’s maybe more because I don’t want to get started down that road of thinking about numbers and whether it’s good or bad. It can make me uncomfortable.\n\nSo… I guess I’d put it more as I get bothered by it sometimes, or I’d rather not focus on it, but I don’t know if I’d call it a constant worry. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It hasn’t stopped me from doing the main things, no. I still go to Mass, and I still go to choir, and I keep up with my house well enough. I mean, maybe not perfectly, but I do the dishes and the laundry and the ordinary things that need doing.

But after one of those afternoons, when I’ve eaten more than I meant to and I feel embarrassed about it, I do sometimes sort of shut myself in. I’ll think, “Well, I’m not calling anybody now,” or if someone comes to mind that I should check on, I’ll put it off. Not because I don’t care, but because I don’t want to talk and sound normal when I’m feeling that way. I might stay home the rest of the day and just keep to myself.

So I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me withdraw a bit afterward. I get ashamed, I guess, and I don’t want anyone noticing.

I don’t sit down in the morning and think, “At three o’clock I’m going to eat this or that.” It doesn’t feel that organized, if that makes sense.\n\nI do think about food in a regular way, you know, like what I need from the store or what I might make, because I’ve always cooked, and cooking has been part of my life for so long. But I don’t know that I spend a lot of time planning when I’ll eat. It’s more that food is just… around in my mind because it’s familiar. I notice it more when I’m alone, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’m making plans for it ahead of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, Doctor. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very often—honestly, I tend to avoid it because it makes me nervous and then I start thinking about it too much. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but I can’t say if that’s my weight changing or just, you know, how clothes fit depending on the day.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve noticed anything I’d call significant, but I wouldn’t want to say for certain without checking. I just haven’t been keeping track of it closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating, even when it's not one of those episodes where you feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I usually feel guilty every time, no. I mean, if it’s just a normal meal, like I make myself some toast or have supper, I don’t sit there afterward thinking I’ve done something terrible. At least not most of the time.\n\nSometimes I might feel a little bothered if I think I ate more than I meant to, even if it wasn’t one of those times where I truly couldn’t stop. But guilt… I’m not sure I’d call it that every time. It’s more like second-guessing myself, maybe. I’ll think, “Did I really need that?” or “Why did I have another serving?” But then other times I just eat and go on with the rest of the evening.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not usually, not after ordinary eating. It’s more confusing than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel like you're not good enough in areas of your life unrelated to eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often, no. I mean, I suppose everybody criticizes themselves a little bit, don’t they? Especially when you get older and you look back on things, you can think, “Well, I could’ve done that better,” or “Maybe I should’ve handled that differently.” I do that sometimes.\n\nBut feeling like I’m not good enough in general… I don’t know. That’s a hard one to answer. I’m not someone who goes around thinking highly of myself, but I also don’t think I spend all day tearing myself down about everything. If I forget something or don’t get something done the way I wanted, I might get annoyed with myself, but it usually passes.\n\nSo I guess I’d say not especially, or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big pattern outside of the eating stuff. It’s more just ordinary second-guessing, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say I often worry about it. I mean, I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. At my age, your body isn’t the same as it used to be, and some days I’ll notice how something fits or I’ll catch sight of myself and think, “Well, I don’t like that very much.” But I don’t know that I sit around worrying about my shape all the time.\n\nI do tend to avoid thinking too hard about my weight, honestly. Like I said, weighing myself makes me uneasy, so I don’t do it much. But that’s maybe more because I don’t want to get started down that road of thinking about numbers and whether it’s good or bad. It can make me uncomfortable.\n\nSo… I guess I’d put it more as I get bothered by it sometimes, or I’d rather not focus on it, but I don’t know if I’d call it a constant worry. It comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused any problems in your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from doing the main things, no. I still go to Mass, and I still go to choir, and I keep up with my house well enough. I mean, maybe not perfectly, but I do the dishes and the laundry and the ordinary things that need doing.\n\nBut after one of those afternoons, when I’ve eaten more than I meant to and I feel embarrassed about it, I do sometimes sort of shut myself in. I’ll think, “Well, I’m not calling anybody now,” or if someone comes to mind that I should check on, I’ll put it off. Not because I don’t care, but because I don’t want to talk and sound normal when I’m feeling that way. I might stay home the rest of the day and just keep to myself.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me withdraw a bit afterward. I get ashamed, I guess, and I don’t want anyone noticing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose I do, though maybe not in a counting-it-all-up sort of way. It’s more that afterward I keep going back over it in my mind, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why couldn’t I just stop?” And then I feel ashamed.

It makes me sad too, because food always used to mean something good to me, you know? Family, taking care of people, holidays, church suppers, bringing something over when someone was sick. It was a way of showing love, really. And then when I have one of those episodes, it feels like I’ve turned that into something secret and ugly, something I have to hide. Especially from people at church. I wouldn’t want them knowing that side of me.

So yes, I do think about it afterward. Not always every minute, but it sits with me. I feel embarrassed and low, and I keep it to myself.

Once I start, it’s like I keep telling myself, “Maria, that is enough now,” but then my hand just goes back to the pot again anyway. I know I should stop, I really do, and I’m thinking it right there in the moment, but it feels like I can’t quite make myself stop. It’s upsetting, and I guess I finally thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been happening most weeks, I’d say. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually once or twice in a week. It got more noticeable after my friend passed away… those quiet afternoons are the hardest, especially after I come home from choir or from working in the garden. I’ll be by myself, and that’s when it tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Afterward, I just feel awful about myself, honestly. I feel ashamed… and sad, too. It’s not only that I ate too much, it’s what it means to me, I suppose. Some of those foods used to feel like family, like caring for people, bringing a dish to church or making something the way my mother did. And now, when it happens like that, it feels like I’ve turned it into something secret and ugly, something I’m hiding from people at church.\n\nI’ll sit there afterward and think, “What is wrong with me, Maria?” And then I feel embarrassed, like if anyone knew, they’d look at me differently. It makes me feel very low.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating too much because you can't control your eating, or is it more that you're eating to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it’s both in a way, but the part that scares me most is the not being able to stop. That’s what brings me here, really. Once I start with those comfort foods, I can be standing right there and saying to myself, Maria, that is enough now. You don’t need any more. And I mean it when I say it. It’s not like I don’t know.\n\nBut then my hand goes back to the pot, or I take another bite, and another, and it feels like I can’t quite make myself stop even though I’m telling myself to. It’s like there’s this little argument going on inside me, and the sensible part of me is losing. So yes, I may be upset or lonely, but in the moment it’s that loss of control that really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about food or planning when you will eat?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m planning it, no. I don’t sit down in the morning and think, “At three o’clock I’m going to eat this or that.” It doesn’t feel that organized, if that makes sense.\n\nI do think about food in a regular way, you know, like what I need from the store or what I might make, because I’ve always cooked, and cooking has been part of my life for so long. But I don’t know that I spend a lot of time planning when I’ll eat. It’s more that food is just… around in my mind because it’s familiar. I notice it more when I’m alone, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’m making plans for it ahead of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, Doctor. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very often—honestly, I tend to avoid it because it makes me nervous and then I start thinking about it too much. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but I can’t say if that’s my weight changing or just, you know, how clothes fit depending on the day.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve noticed anything I’d call significant, but I wouldn’t want to say for certain without checking. I just haven’t been keeping track of it closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating, even when it's not one of those episodes where you feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I usually feel guilty every time, no. I mean, if it’s just a normal meal, like I make myself some toast or have supper, I don’t sit there afterward thinking I’ve done something terrible. At least not most of the time.\n\nSometimes I might feel a little bothered if I think I ate more than I meant to, even if it wasn’t one of those times where I truly couldn’t stop. But guilt… I’m not sure I’d call it that every time. It’s more like second-guessing myself, maybe. I’ll think, “Did I really need that?” or “Why did I have another serving?” But then other times I just eat and go on with the rest of the evening.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not usually, not after ordinary eating. It’s more confusing than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel like you're not good enough in areas of your life unrelated to eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often, no. I mean, I suppose everybody criticizes themselves a little bit, don’t they? Especially when you get older and you look back on things, you can think, “Well, I could’ve done that better,” or “Maybe I should’ve handled that differently.” I do that sometimes.\n\nBut feeling like I’m not good enough in general… I don’t know. That’s a hard one to answer. I’m not someone who goes around thinking highly of myself, but I also don’t think I spend all day tearing myself down about everything. If I forget something or don’t get something done the way I wanted, I might get annoyed with myself, but it usually passes.\n\nSo I guess I’d say not especially, or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big pattern outside of the eating stuff. It’s more just ordinary second-guessing, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say I often worry about it. I mean, I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. At my age, your body isn’t the same as it used to be, and some days I’ll notice how something fits or I’ll catch sight of myself and think, “Well, I don’t like that very much.” But I don’t know that I sit around worrying about my shape all the time.\n\nI do tend to avoid thinking too hard about my weight, honestly. Like I said, weighing myself makes me uneasy, so I don’t do it much. But that’s maybe more because I don’t want to get started down that road of thinking about numbers and whether it’s good or bad. It can make me uncomfortable.\n\nSo… I guess I’d put it more as I get bothered by it sometimes, or I’d rather not focus on it, but I don’t know if I’d call it a constant worry. It comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused any problems in your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from doing the main things, no. I still go to Mass, and I still go to choir, and I keep up with my house well enough. I mean, maybe not perfectly, but I do the dishes and the laundry and the ordinary things that need doing.\n\nBut after one of those afternoons, when I’ve eaten more than I meant to and I feel embarrassed about it, I do sometimes sort of shut myself in. I’ll think, “Well, I’m not calling anybody now,” or if someone comes to mind that I should check on, I’ll put it off. Not because I don’t care, but because I don’t want to talk and sound normal when I’m feeling that way. I might stay home the rest of the day and just keep to myself.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me withdraw a bit afterward. I get ashamed, I guess, and I don’t want anyone noticing.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time thinking about what you've eaten after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though maybe not in a counting-it-all-up sort of way. It’s more that afterward I keep going back over it in my mind, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why couldn’t I just stop?” And then I feel ashamed.\n\nIt makes me sad too, because food always used to mean something good to me, you know? Family, taking care of people, holidays, church suppers, bringing something over when someone was sick. It was a way of showing love, really. And then when I have one of those episodes, it feels like I’ve turned that into something secret and ugly, something I have to hide. Especially from people at church. I wouldn’t want them knowing that side of me.\n\nSo yes, I do think about it afterward. Not always every minute, but it sits with me. I feel embarrassed and low, and I keep it to myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.8", "F50.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I feel embarrassed and low, and I keep it to myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever done anything to try to make up for eating too much, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 78 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I do anything deliberate like that. I don’t go out and exercise extra, no. At my age, I’m not the sort to suddenly take a long walk just because I ate too much. And I don’t really think in those terms, like “I have to burn this off” or anything.

Skipping meals… I don’t know. Sometimes the next morning I might not feel very hungry, or I might just have coffee or something small, but I don’t know that it’s me trying to make up for it exactly. It’s more that I feel a bit put off by the whole thing and don’t want to think about food right away. But I’m not planning it out like punishment, if that makes sense.

Mostly I just feel bad about it afterward and try to get back to my usual routine. I don’t have some special thing I do to undo it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I’ve been having trouble with my eating, mostly with certain comfort foods. Once I start, it’s like I keep telling myself, “Maria, that is enough now,” but then my hand just goes back to the pot again anyway. I know I should stop, I really do, and I’m thinking it right there in the moment, but it feels like I can’t quite make myself stop. It’s upsetting, and I guess I finally thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been happening most weeks, I’d say. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually once or twice in a week. It got more noticeable after my friend passed away… those quiet afternoons are the hardest, especially after I come home from choir or from working in the garden. I’ll be by myself, and that’s when it tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Afterward, I just feel awful about myself, honestly. I feel ashamed… and sad, too. It’s not only that I ate too much, it’s what it means to me, I suppose. Some of those foods used to feel like family, like caring for people, bringing a dish to church or making something the way my mother did. And now, when it happens like that, it feels like I’ve turned it into something secret and ugly, something I’m hiding from people at church.\n\nI’ll sit there afterward and think, “What is wrong with me, Maria?” And then I feel embarrassed, like if anyone knew, they’d look at me differently. It makes me feel very low.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating too much because you can't control your eating, or is it more that you're eating to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it’s both in a way, but the part that scares me most is the not being able to stop. That’s what brings me here, really. Once I start with those comfort foods, I can be standing right there and saying to myself, Maria, that is enough now. You don’t need any more. And I mean it when I say it. It’s not like I don’t know.\n\nBut then my hand goes back to the pot, or I take another bite, and another, and it feels like I can’t quite make myself stop even though I’m telling myself to. It’s like there’s this little argument going on inside me, and the sensible part of me is losing. So yes, I may be upset or lonely, but in the moment it’s that loss of control that really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about food or planning when you will eat?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m planning it, no. I don’t sit down in the morning and think, “At three o’clock I’m going to eat this or that.” It doesn’t feel that organized, if that makes sense.\n\nI do think about food in a regular way, you know, like what I need from the store or what I might make, because I’ve always cooked, and cooking has been part of my life for so long. But I don’t know that I spend a lot of time planning when I’ll eat. It’s more that food is just… around in my mind because it’s familiar. I notice it more when I’m alone, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’m making plans for it ahead of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, Doctor. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very often—honestly, I tend to avoid it because it makes me nervous and then I start thinking about it too much. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but I can’t say if that’s my weight changing or just, you know, how clothes fit depending on the day.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve noticed anything I’d call significant, but I wouldn’t want to say for certain without checking. I just haven’t been keeping track of it closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating, even when it's not one of those episodes where you feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I usually feel guilty every time, no. I mean, if it’s just a normal meal, like I make myself some toast or have supper, I don’t sit there afterward thinking I’ve done something terrible. At least not most of the time.\n\nSometimes I might feel a little bothered if I think I ate more than I meant to, even if it wasn’t one of those times where I truly couldn’t stop. But guilt… I’m not sure I’d call it that every time. It’s more like second-guessing myself, maybe. I’ll think, “Did I really need that?” or “Why did I have another serving?” But then other times I just eat and go on with the rest of the evening.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not usually, not after ordinary eating. It’s more confusing than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel like you're not good enough in areas of your life unrelated to eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often, no. I mean, I suppose everybody criticizes themselves a little bit, don’t they? Especially when you get older and you look back on things, you can think, “Well, I could’ve done that better,” or “Maybe I should’ve handled that differently.” I do that sometimes.\n\nBut feeling like I’m not good enough in general… I don’t know. That’s a hard one to answer. I’m not someone who goes around thinking highly of myself, but I also don’t think I spend all day tearing myself down about everything. If I forget something or don’t get something done the way I wanted, I might get annoyed with myself, but it usually passes.\n\nSo I guess I’d say not especially, or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big pattern outside of the eating stuff. It’s more just ordinary second-guessing, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say I often worry about it. I mean, I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. At my age, your body isn’t the same as it used to be, and some days I’ll notice how something fits or I’ll catch sight of myself and think, “Well, I don’t like that very much.” But I don’t know that I sit around worrying about my shape all the time.\n\nI do tend to avoid thinking too hard about my weight, honestly. Like I said, weighing myself makes me uneasy, so I don’t do it much. But that’s maybe more because I don’t want to get started down that road of thinking about numbers and whether it’s good or bad. It can make me uncomfortable.\n\nSo… I guess I’d put it more as I get bothered by it sometimes, or I’d rather not focus on it, but I don’t know if I’d call it a constant worry. It comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused any problems in your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from doing the main things, no. I still go to Mass, and I still go to choir, and I keep up with my house well enough. I mean, maybe not perfectly, but I do the dishes and the laundry and the ordinary things that need doing.\n\nBut after one of those afternoons, when I’ve eaten more than I meant to and I feel embarrassed about it, I do sometimes sort of shut myself in. I’ll think, “Well, I’m not calling anybody now,” or if someone comes to mind that I should check on, I’ll put it off. Not because I don’t care, but because I don’t want to talk and sound normal when I’m feeling that way. I might stay home the rest of the day and just keep to myself.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me withdraw a bit afterward. I get ashamed, I guess, and I don’t want anyone noticing.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time thinking about what you've eaten after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though maybe not in a counting-it-all-up sort of way. It’s more that afterward I keep going back over it in my mind, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why couldn’t I just stop?” And then I feel ashamed.\n\nIt makes me sad too, because food always used to mean something good to me, you know? Family, taking care of people, holidays, church suppers, bringing something over when someone was sick. It was a way of showing love, really. And then when I have one of those episodes, it feels like I’ve turned that into something secret and ugly, something I have to hide. Especially from people at church. I wouldn’t want them knowing that side of me.\n\nSo yes, I do think about it afterward. Not always every minute, but it sits with me. I feel embarrassed and low, and I keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything to try to make up for eating too much, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I do anything deliberate like that. I don’t go out and exercise extra, no. At my age, I’m not the sort to suddenly take a long walk just because I ate too much. And I don’t really think in those terms, like “I have to burn this off” or anything.\n\nSkipping meals… I don’t know. Sometimes the next morning I might not feel very hungry, or I might just have coffee or something small, but I don’t know that it’s me trying to make up for it exactly. It’s more that I feel a bit put off by the whole thing and don’t want to think about food right away. But I’m not planning it out like punishment, if that makes sense.\n\nMostly I just feel bad about it afterward and try to get back to my usual routine. I don’t have some special thing I do to undo it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I wouldn’t want them knowing that side of me.\n\nSo yes, I do think about it afterward. Not always every minute, but it sits with me. I feel embarrassed and low, and I keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything to try to make up for eating too much, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I do anything deliberate like that. I don’t go out and exercise extra, no. At my age, I’m not the sort to suddenly take a long walk just because I ate too much. And I don’t really think in those terms, like “I have to burn this off” or anything.\n\nSkipping meals… I don’t know. Sometimes the next morning I might not feel very hungry, or I might just have coffee or something small, but I don’t know that it’s me trying to make up for it exactly. It’s more that I feel a bit put off by the whole thing and don’t want to think about food right away. But I’m not planning it out like punishment, if that makes sense.\n\nMostly I just feel bad about it afterward and try to get back to my usual routine. I don’t have some special thing I do to undo it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you spend more than an hour a day thinking about food or planning meals?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 13\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question asks about spending more than an hour per day thinking about food or planning meals, but the provided symptom profile does not include a symptom item for daily food preoccupation or meal planning."}

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused any problems in your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from doing the main things, no. I still go to Mass, and I still go to choir, and I keep up with my house well enough. I mean, maybe not perfectly, but I do the dishes and the laundry and the ordinary things that need doing.\n\nBut after one of those afternoons, when I’ve eaten more than I meant to and I feel embarrassed about it, I do sometimes sort of shut myself in. I’ll think, “Well, I’m not calling anybody now,” or if someone comes to mind that I should check on, I’ll put it off. Not because I don’t care, but because I don’t want to talk and sound normal when I’m feeling that way. I might stay home the rest of the day and just keep to myself.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me withdraw a bit afterward. I get ashamed, I guess, and I don’t want anyone noticing.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time thinking about what you've eaten after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though maybe not in a counting-it-all-up sort of way. It’s more that afterward I keep going back over it in my mind, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why couldn’t I just stop?” And then I feel ashamed.\n\nIt makes me sad too, because food always used to mean something good to me, you know? Family, taking care of people, holidays, church suppers, bringing something over when someone was sick. It was a way of showing love, really. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More than an hour a day… I don’t think so, no. I mean, I do think about food in an ordinary way, like what I’m going to have for supper, or if I need to use something before it spoils, or if there’s something at church where I might bring a dish. That’s always been part of my day, really, especially living alone and having to plan for myself.

But if you mean sitting there for hours planning meals or going over food in my head all day, I don’t think it’s like that. Some days I barely plan at all and just have whatever is easy. Other days I might think ahead a bit, especially if I’m going to the store, but I wouldn’t say it takes up that much time most days. It’s not like I’m writing menus or anything.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I’ve been having trouble with my eating, mostly with certain comfort foods. Once I start, it’s like I keep telling myself, “Maria, that is enough now,” but then my hand just goes back to the pot again anyway. I know I should stop, I really do, and I’m thinking it right there in the moment, but it feels like I can’t quite make myself stop. It’s upsetting, and I guess I finally thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been happening most weeks, I’d say. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually once or twice in a week. It got more noticeable after my friend passed away… those quiet afternoons are the hardest, especially after I come home from choir or from working in the garden. I’ll be by myself, and that’s when it tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Afterward, I just feel awful about myself, honestly. I feel ashamed… and sad, too. It’s not only that I ate too much, it’s what it means to me, I suppose. Some of those foods used to feel like family, like caring for people, bringing a dish to church or making something the way my mother did. And now, when it happens like that, it feels like I’ve turned it into something secret and ugly, something I’m hiding from people at church.\n\nI’ll sit there afterward and think, “What is wrong with me, Maria?” And then I feel embarrassed, like if anyone knew, they’d look at me differently. It makes me feel very low.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating too much because you can't control your eating, or is it more that you're eating to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it’s both in a way, but the part that scares me most is the not being able to stop. That’s what brings me here, really. Once I start with those comfort foods, I can be standing right there and saying to myself, Maria, that is enough now. You don’t need any more. And I mean it when I say it. It’s not like I don’t know.\n\nBut then my hand goes back to the pot, or I take another bite, and another, and it feels like I can’t quite make myself stop even though I’m telling myself to. It’s like there’s this little argument going on inside me, and the sensible part of me is losing. So yes, I may be upset or lonely, but in the moment it’s that loss of control that really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about food or planning when you will eat?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m planning it, no. I don’t sit down in the morning and think, “At three o’clock I’m going to eat this or that.” It doesn’t feel that organized, if that makes sense.\n\nI do think about food in a regular way, you know, like what I need from the store or what I might make, because I’ve always cooked, and cooking has been part of my life for so long. But I don’t know that I spend a lot of time planning when I’ll eat. It’s more that food is just… around in my mind because it’s familiar. I notice it more when I’m alone, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’m making plans for it ahead of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, Doctor. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very often—honestly, I tend to avoid it because it makes me nervous and then I start thinking about it too much. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but I can’t say if that’s my weight changing or just, you know, how clothes fit depending on the day.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve noticed anything I’d call significant, but I wouldn’t want to say for certain without checking. I just haven’t been keeping track of it closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating, even when it's not one of those episodes where you feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I usually feel guilty every time, no. I mean, if it’s just a normal meal, like I make myself some toast or have supper, I don’t sit there afterward thinking I’ve done something terrible. At least not most of the time.\n\nSometimes I might feel a little bothered if I think I ate more than I meant to, even if it wasn’t one of those times where I truly couldn’t stop. But guilt… I’m not sure I’d call it that every time. It’s more like second-guessing myself, maybe. I’ll think, “Did I really need that?” or “Why did I have another serving?” But then other times I just eat and go on with the rest of the evening.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not usually, not after ordinary eating. It’s more confusing than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel like you're not good enough in areas of your life unrelated to eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often, no. I mean, I suppose everybody criticizes themselves a little bit, don’t they? Especially when you get older and you look back on things, you can think, “Well, I could’ve done that better,” or “Maybe I should’ve handled that differently.” I do that sometimes.\n\nBut feeling like I’m not good enough in general… I don’t know. That’s a hard one to answer. I’m not someone who goes around thinking highly of myself, but I also don’t think I spend all day tearing myself down about everything. If I forget something or don’t get something done the way I wanted, I might get annoyed with myself, but it usually passes.\n\nSo I guess I’d say not especially, or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big pattern outside of the eating stuff. It’s more just ordinary second-guessing, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say I often worry about it. I mean, I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. At my age, your body isn’t the same as it used to be, and some days I’ll notice how something fits or I’ll catch sight of myself and think, “Well, I don’t like that very much.” But I don’t know that I sit around worrying about my shape all the time.\n\nI do tend to avoid thinking too hard about my weight, honestly. Like I said, weighing myself makes me uneasy, so I don’t do it much. But that’s maybe more because I don’t want to get started down that road of thinking about numbers and whether it’s good or bad. It can make me uncomfortable.\n\nSo… I guess I’d put it more as I get bothered by it sometimes, or I’d rather not focus on it, but I don’t know if I’d call it a constant worry. It comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused any problems in your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from doing the main things, no. I still go to Mass, and I still go to choir, and I keep up with my house well enough. I mean, maybe not perfectly, but I do the dishes and the laundry and the ordinary things that need doing.\n\nBut after one of those afternoons, when I’ve eaten more than I meant to and I feel embarrassed about it, I do sometimes sort of shut myself in. I’ll think, “Well, I’m not calling anybody now,” or if someone comes to mind that I should check on, I’ll put it off. Not because I don’t care, but because I don’t want to talk and sound normal when I’m feeling that way. I might stay home the rest of the day and just keep to myself.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me withdraw a bit afterward. I get ashamed, I guess, and I don’t want anyone noticing.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time thinking about what you've eaten after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though maybe not in a counting-it-all-up sort of way. It’s more that afterward I keep going back over it in my mind, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why couldn’t I just stop?” And then I feel ashamed.\n\nIt makes me sad too, because food always used to mean something good to me, you know? Family, taking care of people, holidays, church suppers, bringing something over when someone was sick. It was a way of showing love, really. And then when I have one of those episodes, it feels like I’ve turned that into something secret and ugly, something I have to hide. Especially from people at church. I wouldn’t want them knowing that side of me.\n\nSo yes, I do think about it afterward. Not always every minute, but it sits with me. I feel embarrassed and low, and I keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything to try to make up for eating too much, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I do anything deliberate like that. I don’t go out and exercise extra, no. At my age, I’m not the sort to suddenly take a long walk just because I ate too much. And I don’t really think in those terms, like “I have to burn this off” or anything.\n\nSkipping meals… I don’t know. Sometimes the next morning I might not feel very hungry, or I might just have coffee or something small, but I don’t know that it’s me trying to make up for it exactly. But I’m not planning it out like punishment, if that makes sense.\n\nMostly I just feel bad about it afterward and try to get back to my usual routine. I don’t have some special thing I do to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend more than an hour a day thinking about food or planning meals?\n\nPatient: More than an hour a day… I don’t think so, no. I mean, I do think about food in an ordinary way, like what I’m going to have for supper, or if I need to use something before it spoils, or if there’s something at church where I might bring a dish. That’s always been part of my day, really, especially living alone and having to plan for myself.\n\nBut if you mean sitting there for hours planning meals or going over food in my head all day, I don’t think it’s like that. It’s not like I’m writing menus or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Hmm… unrelated to eating. I’m not sure I’ve really kept track of that. I suppose I do criticize myself sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people do.

It’s upsetting, and I guess I finally thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been happening most weeks, I’d say. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually once or twice in a week. It got more noticeable after my friend passed away… those quiet afternoons are the hardest, especially after I come home from choir or from working in the garden. It makes me feel very low.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating too much because you can't control your eating, or is it more that you're eating to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it’s both in a way, but the part that scares me most is the not being able to stop. So yes, I may be upset or lonely, but in the moment it’s that loss of control that really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about food or planning when you will eat?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m planning it, no. I don’t sit down in the morning and think, “At three o’clock I’m going to eat this or that.” It doesn’t feel that organized, if that makes sense.\n\nI do think about food in a regular way, you know, like what I need from the store or what I might make, because I’ve always cooked, and cooking has been part of my life for so long. But I don’t know that I spend a lot of time planning when I’ll eat. It’s more that food is just… around in my mind because it’s familiar. I notice it more when I’m alone, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’m making plans for it ahead of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, Doctor. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very often—honestly, I tend to avoid it because it makes me nervous and then I start thinking about it too much. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but I can’t say if that’s my weight changing or just, you know, how clothes fit depending on the day.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve noticed anything I’d call significant, but I wouldn’t want to say for certain without checking. I just haven’t been keeping track of it closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating, even when it's not one of those episodes where you feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I usually feel guilty every time, no. I mean, if it’s just a normal meal, like I make myself some toast or have supper, I don’t sit there afterward thinking I’ve done something terrible. At least not most of the time.\n\nSometimes I might feel a little bothered if I think I ate more than I meant to, even if it wasn’t one of those times where I truly couldn’t stop. But guilt… I’m not sure I’d call it that every time. It’s more like second-guessing myself, maybe. I’ll think, “Did I really need that?” or “Why did I have another serving?” But then other times I just eat and go on with the rest of the evening.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not usually, not after ordinary eating. It’s more confusing than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel like you're not good enough in areas of your life unrelated to eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often, no. I mean, I suppose everybody criticizes themselves a little bit, don’t they? Especially when you get older and you look back on things, you can think, “Well, I could’ve done that better,” or “Maybe I should’ve handled that differently.” I do that sometimes.\n\nBut feeling like I’m not good enough in general… I don’t know. That’s a hard one to answer. I’m not someone who goes around thinking highly of myself, but I also don’t think I spend all day tearing myself down about everything. If I forget something or don’t get something done the way I wanted, I might get annoyed with myself, but it usually passes.\n\nSo I guess I’d say not especially, or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big pattern outside of the eating stuff. It’s more just ordinary second-guessing, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say I often worry about it. I mean, I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. At my age, your body isn’t the same as it used to be, and some days I’ll notice how something fits or I’ll catch sight of myself and think, “Well, I don’t like that very much.” But I don’t know that I sit around worrying about my shape all the time.\n\nI do tend to avoid thinking too hard about my weight, honestly. Like I said, weighing myself makes me uneasy, so I don’t do it much. But that’s maybe more because I don’t want to get started down that road of thinking about numbers and whether it’s good or bad. It comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused any problems in your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from doing the main things, no. I still go to Mass, and I still go to choir, and I keep up with my house well enough. I mean, maybe not perfectly, but I do the dishes and the laundry and the ordinary things that need doing.\n\nBut after one of those afternoons, when I’ve eaten more than I meant to and I feel embarrassed about it, I do sometimes sort of shut myself in. Not because I don’t care, but because I don’t want to talk and sound normal when I’m feeling that way. I might stay home the rest of the day and just keep to myself.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me withdraw a bit afterward. I get ashamed, I guess, and I don’t want anyone noticing.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time thinking about what you've eaten after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though maybe not in a counting-it-all-up sort of way. It got more noticeable after my friend passed away… those quiet afternoons are the hardest, especially after I come home from choir or from working in the garden. I’ll be by myself, and that’s when it tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Afterward, I just feel awful about myself, honestly. I feel ashamed… and sad, too. It’s not only that I ate too much, it’s what it means to me, I suppose. Some of those foods used to feel like family, like caring for people, bringing a dish to church or making something the way my mother did. And now, when it happens like that, it feels like I’ve turned it into something secret and ugly, something I’m hiding from people at church.\n\nI’ll sit there afterward and think, “What is wrong with me, Maria?” And then I feel embarrassed, like if anyone knew, they’d look at me differently. It makes me feel very low.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating too much because you can't control your eating, or is it more that you're eating to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it’s both in a way, but the part that scares me most is the not being able to stop. That’s what brings me here, really. Once I start with those comfort foods, I can be standing right there and saying to myself, Maria, that is enough now. You don’t need any more. And I mean it when I say it. It’s not like I don’t know.\n\nBut then my hand goes back to the pot, or I take another bite, and another, and it feels like I can’t quite make myself stop even though I’m telling myself to. It’s like there’s this little argument going on inside me, and the sensible part of me is losing. So yes, I may be upset or lonely, but in the moment it’s that loss of control that really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about food or planning when you will eat?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m planning it, no. I don’t sit down in the morning and think, “At three o’clock I’m going to eat this or that.” It doesn’t feel that organized, if that makes sense.\n\nI do think about food in a regular way, you know, like what I need from the store or what I might make, because I’ve always cooked, and cooking has been part of my life for so long. But I don’t know that I spend a lot of time planning when I’ll eat. It’s more that food is just… around in my mind because it’s familiar. I notice it more when I’m alone, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’m making plans for it ahead of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, Doctor. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very often—honestly, I tend to avoid it because it makes me nervous and then I start thinking about it too much. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but I can’t say if that’s my weight changing or just, you know, how clothes fit depending on the day.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve noticed anything I’d call significant, but I wouldn’t want to say for certain without checking. I just haven’t been keeping track of it closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating, even when it's not one of those episodes where you feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I usually feel guilty every time, no. I mean, if it’s just a normal meal, like I make myself some toast or have supper, I don’t sit there afterward thinking I’ve done something terrible. At least not most of the time.\n\nSometimes I might feel a little bothered if I think I ate more than I meant to, even if it wasn’t one of those times where I truly couldn’t stop. But guilt… I’m not sure I’d call it that every time. It’s more like second-guessing myself, maybe. I’ll think, “Did I really need that?” or “Why did I have another serving?” But then other times I just eat and go on with the rest of the evening.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not usually, not after ordinary eating. It’s more confusing than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel like you're not good enough in areas of your life unrelated to eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often, no. I mean, I suppose everybody criticizes themselves a little bit, don’t they? Especially when you get older and you look back on things, you can think, “Well, I could’ve done that better,” or “Maybe I should’ve handled that differently.” I do that sometimes.\n\nBut feeling like I’m not good enough in general… I don’t know. That’s a hard one to answer. I’m not someone who goes around thinking highly of myself, but I also don’t think I spend all day tearing myself down about everything. If I forget something or don’t get something done the way I wanted, I might get annoyed with myself, but it usually passes.\n\nSo I guess I’d say not especially, or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big pattern outside of the eating stuff. It’s more just ordinary second-guessing, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say I often worry about it. I mean, I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. At my age, your body isn’t the same as it used to be, and some days I’ll notice how something fits or I’ll catch sight of myself and think, “Well, I don’t like that very much.” But I don’t know that I sit around worrying about my shape all the time.\n\nI do tend to avoid thinking too hard about my weight, honestly. Like I said, weighing myself makes me uneasy, so I don’t do it much. But that’s maybe more because I don’t want to get started down that road of thinking about numbers and whether it’s good or bad. It can make me uncomfortable.\n\nSo… I guess I’d put it more as I get bothered by it sometimes, or I’d rather not focus on it, but I don’t know if I’d call it a constant worry. It comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused any problems in your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from doing the main things, no. I still go to Mass, and I still go to choir, and I keep up with my house well enough. I mean, maybe not perfectly, but I do the dishes and the laundry and the ordinary things that need doing.\n\nBut after one of those afternoons, when I’ve eaten more than I meant to and I feel embarrassed about it, I do sometimes sort of shut myself in. I’ll think, “Well, I’m not calling anybody now,” or if someone comes to mind that I should check on, I’ll put it off. Not because I don’t care, but because I don’t want to talk and sound normal when I’m feeling that way. I might stay home the rest of the day and just keep to myself.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me withdraw a bit afterward. I get ashamed, I guess, and I don’t want anyone noticing.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time thinking about what you've eaten after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though maybe not in a counting-it-all-up sort of way. It’s more that afterward I keep going back over it in my mind, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why couldn’t I just stop?” And then I feel ashamed.\n\nIt makes me sad too, because food always used to mean something good to me, you know? Family, taking care of people, holidays, church suppers, bringing something over when someone was sick. It was a way of showing love, really. And then when I have one of those episodes, it feels like I’ve turned that into something secret and ugly, something I have to hide. Especially from people at church. I wouldn’t want them knowing that side of me.\n\nSo yes, I do think about it afterward. Not always every minute, but it sits with me. I feel embarrassed and low, and I keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything to try to make up for eating too much, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I do anything deliberate like that. I don’t go out and exercise extra, no. At my age, I’m not the sort to suddenly take a long walk just because I ate too much. And I don’t really think in those terms, like “I have to burn this off” or anything.\n\nSkipping meals… I don’t know. Sometimes the next morning I might not feel very hungry, or I might just have coffee or something small, but I don’t know that it’s me trying to make up for it exactly. It’s more that I feel a bit put off by the whole thing and don’t want to think about food right away. But I’m not planning it out like punishment, if that makes sense.\n\nMostly I just feel bad about it afterward and try to get back to my usual routine. I don’t have some special thing I do to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend more than an hour a day thinking about food or planning meals?\n\nPatient: More than an hour a day… I don’t think so, no. I mean, I do think about food in an ordinary way, like what I’m going to have for supper, or if I need to use something before it spoils, or if there’s something at church where I might bring a dish. That’s always been part of my day, really, especially living alone and having to plan for myself.\n\nBut if you mean sitting there for hours planning meals or going over food in my head all day, I don’t think it’s like that. Some days I barely plan at all and just have whatever is easy. Other days I might think ahead a bit, especially if I’m going to the store, but I wouldn’t say it takes up that much time most days. It’s not like I’m writing menus or anything.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself criticizing yourself for things unrelated to eating?\n\nPatient: Hmm… unrelated to eating. I’m not sure I’ve really kept track of that. I suppose I do criticize myself sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people do.\n\nI might get after myself for forgetting something, or not getting around to a chore, or saying something awkward and then thinking about it later. Little things like that. I’ll think, “Well, that was foolish,” or “Why didn’t you handle that better?” But I can’t say how often exactly.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve sat down and measured, you know. Some days I’m harder on myself than others. I think I’ve always had a bit of that in me, wanting to do things properly and not be a bother to anyone. But I don’t know that I could give you a number.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
